We also reintroduce tax and tax exemptions for gifts so that employers can support local businesses by giving tax-free gifts to their employees. The conversion aid and the turnover aid to individual traders and trading companies will be extended until June and the increased conversion aid for particularly affected companies will now be extended to April 2021. In the summer of 2018, approximately 76,000 children participated in an activity that was financed in full or in part through the state grant for holiday activities. In 2020, most municipalities reduced their summer holiday activities. Of the 229 municipalities that responded to a survey, 169 reported that the lack of support led to fewer activities. 17 municipalities organised nothing at all last year. In the municipalities that organised activities, several activities were subject to a fee, as in Sorsele, where the municipality introduced a fee of SEK 500 for the swimming school. During the Coronasummer 2020 there were municipalities that could not arrange holiday activities without the state support. This year, the municipalities can again organise activities for children and young people, as this report provides funding for praise activities. It is a welcome addition for our municipalities and not least for our children and young people. It is also a good thing that the remuneration of risk groups is now being extended without any gap. The gap that occurred at the turn of the year created major problems for the risk groups. Here, therefore, is my appeal to the government: do not let it be a gap again that forces people to work with covidsickness even though they themselves belong to the risk groups! Although we hope, of course, that everyone is vaccinated and that risks will decrease in the future, an extension to 30 June is relatively short, and any further extension must be made before the summer. In this supplementary amending budget, Parliament also decides to extend the public health authority's possibility to borrow in order to participate in the EU's vaccine procurement for 2022 and 2023. That's good. We need safe vaccines for years to come. However, the EU must work to reduce the cost of the vaccine. We have been able to see how countries in the EU have relied on the cheapest vaccine and have only bought it, which has affected other Member States when that vaccine has been paused. The price plays a role in the availability of vaccines, both within and outside the EU. Both Sweden and the EU should say yes to the petitions made that countries and companies should be able to move away from intellectual property rights temporarily in order to import or manufacture vaccines, medicines and tests for covid-19 during the pandemic. It is far too slow to obtain vaccines, and it is the rich countries, such as Sweden, that have the opportunity to buy the expensive vaccine. The EU should do and can do more to push prices and make it easier for poorer countries to obtain vaccines. In this context, it is also worth mentioning that the EU, Sweden and other countries have contributed to the funding of research on vaccines and covid-19. This funding should also be reflected in pricing. There is an infinite difference between the situation we have today. A few years ago an investigation was conducted by Krister Bringéus. He stated that a Swedish NATO membership would increase predictability and security for both Sweden and our neighbours, because we build security together. The defence committee has pointed out that if something happens in the Baltic countries, Sweden will be affected from day one. We cannot be neutral or stand outside, but we have an obligation to do so. Every time I visit a Baltic country, I hear clearly that Swedish membership would be a great help for common security. The government should listen to this. Firstly, it concerns the NATO option, but also NATO membership. We are close to allies today, have a partnership and are a gold member, but it is time to upgrade it to full membership so that we can get information, full defence cooperation and joint defence planning. This would increase the security of both the Swedish people and our surrounding world. It is time for the government to invite all parties in parliament to talks to discuss the security policy line so that it becomes clear and is supported by far more than the third that is currently behind the government's line.

Safeguarding human security requires policies that can respond to a wide range of security threats. Cooperation is needed. It is important to make a distinction between cooperating and forming part of a military alliance with common defence obligations. We believe that our freedom of alliance serves us well. Being free from binding military alliances increases our ability to engage in dialogue and act as a peace broker, thereby contributing to detour, not least in our neighbourhood. The Green Party therefore says no to membership, because it would restrict our political freedom of action. We are also seeing worrying developments in Turkey, the NATO country, where human rights are increasingly being ignored. Sweden's security is best built through long-termness and clarity. Sweden should therefore not, as several bourgeois parties want, obtain an option to join NATO. A NATO option sends unclear signals to the outside world and disrupts the stability that Sweden stands for in our geopolitical area – a stability that we believe it is of great importance to maintain in order for us to be a peace builder and broker in the world. It must be clear to the outside world that Sweden is and continues to want to be a military non-aligned state. The Green Party, on the other hand, sees the great importance of cooperating with democratic countries in the world. The knowledge that our modern societies build is spread throughout the world. To meet threats to climate, environment and democracy and to preserve peace, open cooperation within the UN and other international bodies is required – cooperation based on human rights and free economy within nature's framework, sharing skills and free research. The strength of the world's democracy is based on this, and we shall defend it. I support the proposal in the report but also the reservation 4 of the Socialist, Left and Green Party in paragraph 4.

As an overall long-term goal, we therefore want to see a world where you can live safely in all parts of the world. Of course, we need a defence, particularly with a focus on crisis and disaster management, but also on military threats, for a long time. However, it is not worth having more weapons than you think you need from, for example, the defense committee's conclusions.

i-f2999c5907a82396-399	Madam President, I would like The security policy situation in Sweden's neighbourhood and in Europe has deteriorated over time. Armed attack on Sweden cannot be ruled out. Nor can it be excluded that military force or threats of such might be used against Sweden. Both on Crimea and on the Russian border in eastern Ukraine, a very worrying construction of military units is taking place today. The situation escalates step by step, and this exercise of power is unacceptable. Russian manoeuvring confirms the image that Moscow is prepared to use military power when it is considered beneficial to its purposes even if it is contrary to international law. We do not accept Russian action and the way in which it tries to dominate the region around Ukraine. Ukraine as a sovereign state shall be respected. The Russian annexation of Crimea will not be more acceptable just because time passes. It is a violation of international law and therefore justifies continued sanctions. Russia has a primary responsibility to defuse military and security policy tensions in Eastern Ukraine and Crimea. Russia bears a clear responsibility for this. Today's situation has a strong impact on Ukrainian society as a whole. And in Russian action there is always an underlying threat of escalation. What is happening in Ukraine today also affects Sweden's and the Nordic countries' security policy situation. Some time ago, I had a bilateral meeting with the Ukrainian Minister of Defence where we confirmed and agreed to continue developing our military cooperation, which has now been going on for about 20 years. In these difficult times, the message must be clear. The territorial integrity of Ukraine must be respected. We distance ourselves from the Russian provocation. Our solidarity with Ukraine is clear and clear. In our neighbourhood we can see reinforcements of both the Russian Baltic Sea Marine and the Northern Marine. These are investments which today enable Russia to operate in the North Atlantic on the basis of a pattern dating back from the Cold War. The exercises and operations may aim to cut supply lines between the US and Europe in a possible conflict, but also to clarify Russian presence in the Arctic region. On the Swedish west coast there has been an increase in activity, and Russian marines operate right up to the UK's territorial water border. The impact area has expanded, providing evidence of increased ambitions and increasing demands on many nations. Sweden's security policy situation is also affected by hybrid threats, cyber attacks, cross-border terrorism and climate change, and all of this has an impact on peace and security. The direction of defence policy must be based on the changing security policy environment, and this is reflected in the last two defence decisions. Sweden will inevitably be affected if a security policy crisis or armed conflict occurs in our neighbourhood. We have given a clear Nordic declaration of solidarity, and we have a clear declaration of solidarity within the EU. The defence cooperation and the security policy cooperation we are building are clearly presented both in the public debate and in the Riksdag. They are based on broad political decisions and have support in defence decisions. I am not speculating on what a possible conflict situation would be like and how different countries would then act, should we unfortunately end up in such a situation – it should be avoided at all costs. However, the interoperability of exercise activities and the currently planned, coordinated planning with NATO, the US, the UK, Norway and Denmark are nevertheless signals that we are looking at various opportunities to build our security network in our part of Europe stronger. Our strategy has two legs. One is enhanced military capability. There we have set aside a considerable amount of money: a 25% increase over the previous defence-oriented period of the budget and a 40% increase up to 2025. We are engaged in extensive defence and security policy cooperation with other states. I am extremely proud that we have reached that point. The basis for this was laid in the Swedish Defence Committee's work on the proposal presented in 2014 and confirmed by the Riksdag in 2015. That is what has been the basis for the government's work on this. Various actors can, of course, describe their individual efforts as crucial. But that is the basis, and that is what has guided the action of the government. This has certainly been necessary in order to build a safer neighbourhood where Sweden is today an active and respected player. The EU is our most important foreign and security policy platform. The starting point is that Europe needs to take greater responsibility for its own security in a way that strengthens both the EU and its Member States. At the same time, cooperation is also about developing work with strategic partners. It includes security policy dialogue, capability development and international crisis management operations. Cooperation in the field of defence can take place in different ways and to varying degrees. Finland, of course, has a special position here and is our deepest cooperation where we also have operational capabilities and operational planning beyond peace-time conditions. However, as the letter says, we also have various forms of agreements, declarations of intent, cooperation programmes and activity lists with the United States, Denmark, Norway, Germany, the United Kingdom and France. The Joint Expeditionary Force is also a very important collaboration. Security policy dialogue is central in this context. Regular meetings, where we can have a security and defence policy dialogue at ministerial level, are a key issue. We also have a well-developed dialogue with all our neighbours around the Baltic Sea and with the USA, the UK and France. Nordefco, JEF, the Northern Group and the European Intervention Initiative are examples of multilateral fora that provide the opportunity for such dialogue, for exchange of experience and for collaboration on strategic communication. Defence cooperation often aims at interoperability, the ability to act together, both internationally and in the neighbourhood. Scenario-based discussions are important. Exercise activities are important, and military capability development is important. We conduct scenario-based discussions with Finland, Norway and Denmark within the framework of Nordefco and the Northern Group. This means that we are jointly preparing to deal with crisis situations. It also raises the level and increases knowledge of the national starting points of different countries. It is then combined with the exercise activities carried out, such as Arctic Challenge Exercise, Cross Border Training, Northern Wind, Baltops, Northern Coasts, Au rora and Cold Response. Joint participation in international efforts is also an important part of defence cooperation. It is an integral part of the solidarity policy on security. These actions take place within the framework of the UN, the EU, NATO, the OSSE and ad hoc coalitions on the basis of mandates in international law. This is extremely important to us. Participation in international efforts also has a direct impact on our bi- and multilateral defence cooperation. We work closely with France, the United Kingdom, the United States and Canada in the context of these efforts. Allowing joint operational action at different levels of conflict is also an objective of cooperation. We have said before that the most far-reaching thing is with Finland. In the total defence bill for 2021 – 2025, we underline the willingness to coordinate operational planning with Norway, Denmark, the United States, the United Kingdom and NATO. The deepening is about creating a truly functioning cooperation with freedom of action in the event of a crisis or conflict. It is a logical consequence of our already close relations but also of the security policy changes that can come quickly. At the same time, cooperation contributes to further stability. They raise the security policy threshold and create the opportunity to deal with rapid and unforeseen developments. An important step in this work is the trilateral declaration of intent to take together an increased responsibility for Nordkalotten that Finland, Sweden and Norway established last autumn at a meeting in Porsangmoen, Norway. Cooperation on materials, research and development is a means of ensuring knowledge, technology and material supply in the bilateral and multilateral format. Materiel cooperation also strengthens the domestic industrial base and contributes to the rational supply of materials throughout the life of the material. Building a security policy architecture that lasts over time is a long-term project. It creates stability and is a very delicate task. Sweden comes from a history that has been about military alliance freedom aimed at neutrality in war. Nowadays it is a question of military alliance freedom. Sweden's security policy line is solid. We're not in any military alliance. Sweden builds security together with others. The joint security policy is the basis of Swedish defence and security policy. This requires an active, broad and responsible foreign and security policy combined with enhanced defence cooperation, especially with Finland, and a credible national defence objective. We have built the international collaborations that have been necessary, and we have done so with the support of the Swedish Parliament. We build interoperability in everyday life, that is, the ability to do something together if necessary. This applies to aircraft as well as marine and army. The operational planning that we have with Finland is not a show bread, but it is a joint planning that can be used in the event that it becomes necessary, if both governments so decide. Since 2014, relations with both Finland and the United States have deepened. The same applies to the relationship with Britain. We wrote a Statement of Intent with the US in 2016. In 2017, Sweden, the United States and Finland wrote a Statement of Intent. This also means that the relationship between Sweden, Finland and the United States has been strengthened. I think that is also an important signal. We are now pleased that the new administration in the United States is strengthening links with Europe. But during the last administration in the United States, we also developed our military cooperation. The commitments that we had agreed with the United States were kept, and they were implemented. The agreements that have been concluded and the contacts that we have made have an important role to play in increasing joint activity. Together with other states and organisations, Sweden is actively contributing in a defence policy context that raises the conflict-resisting threshold. It's really the core of the whole thing. When we build the security network together, train and exchange information and communicate strategically together in our part of Europe and with partners, we raise the threshold for potential antagonists and for a possible conflict situation. In this way, we create more stability that benefits peace in the time in which we live. I believe that Sweden today, with its first increased military capability and, secondly, increased international cooperation, has a realistic strategy without political adventure for how to deal with the situation we have today in our part of Europe.

i-f2999c5907a82396-522	Madam President, I would like Let me start by clarifying two things. Firstly, we Moderates support and want to further develop the bilateral and multilateral defence cooperation outlined in the report. They raise the operational capability of the Swedish Armed Forces. It strengthens our security, and that is basically good. However, none of these defence cooperations include binding defence guarantees in which we can safely know that we are getting help and support in the event of crisis and conflict. They cannot therefore replace the value of full Swedish NATO membership. A Swedish NATO membership would increase our war-resisting capacity and contribute to stability and security in the immediate region. It has been the single largest driving force when it comes to Sweden's development of stronger international defence cooperation. But what is now happening in Ukraine is also a reminder of how central transatlantic cooperation and NATO are to Europe's security. Basically, this is all about facilitating defence cooperation if you have systemic equality. It strengthens and coordinates our security policy cooperation, but it also ensures that we have equal relations between Sweden and Finland in our relationship with NATO. I noticed that several people asked: What does a Swedish NATO option mean in practice? Yes, it is basically three things: that Sweden clearly states that it maintains the possibility and freedom of action to seek membership of NATO, that Sweden is paying attention to changes in the security environment that can affect such a decision, and that Sweden ensures that there are no practical or technical obstacles that could hinder accession. In my view, this should be completely undramatic. I cannot understand the government's strong opposition to this. It seems almost incomprehensible. It seems that one is caught up in some kind of identity policy when it comes to the military freedom of alliance and the NATO issue. I think that this could be resolved very pragmatically. The arguments put forward by the government against a NATO option are that they want to avoid sharp throws and that they do not want to do anything that undermines military alliance freedom. Madam President, I would like A Swedish NATO option is a marginal shift of the security policy line compared to what the government itself has done in practice over the past four years. Let me take a few examples. Sweden has had a host country agreement with NATO for four years to enable us to receive military support in our territory. Sweden will coordinate its defence planning with NATO and, bilaterally, with the United Kingdom, Denmark, Norway and the United States. Our entire war and basic organisation is based on the fact that we will be supported by NATO countries in the event of an armed attack. The Secretary of Defense has been very clear about where we put regiments so that we can get support from NATO countries. In the light of these radical changes in Swedish security policy in practice, I see a Swedish NATO option as a small step to take. Such a discussion must be based on a serious analysis of our military geography in northern Europe, the realities it entails and the Swedish interests that we have to manage in this. i-f2999c5907a82396-599	Madam President, I would like Inequality and growing economic gaps are a major social problem. There is a strong link between inequality and a variety of social and health indicators. In unequal societies, class and status are becoming more important, and that affects how we people view each other. More and more economists and institutions have also recently shown that increased inequality has a negative impact on economic growth. Sweden is the country, together with New Zealand, where inequality has increased most in the Western world in recent decades. From being the most equal country in the OECD, we were in the ninth place in 2019. While life expectancy increases for high-income and highly educated people, it decreases for women in typical LO professions. The Left Party agrees with the Government that it is important to invest in research into health gaps, but also notes that research already shows how the gaps in society affect health. We can also note that, despite this knowledge, the gaps continue to increase and the government's policy of favouring the rich with tax deductions, tax cuts and other things that drive the development towards greater inequality. If social research is to be useful, policies must also take on board research results and not make decisions based on decisions that run counter to research. Inequality and inequalities in society affect so much more than just the health aspects. Sweden has much to gain by creating an equal society. The challenges of the cleavage society are not addressed in one word, but are hidden behind words such as socio-economic segregation and socio-economic challenges. Therefore, the Left Party proposes, among other things, that the proposed research programme on segregation should have the focus on segregation, class and equal conditions. We must dare to talk about class! Madam President, I would like Sweden is a research nation. We are at the forefront, but it is also clear that we are losing towards other countries. Is this a research bill that will change that? No, I don't think so. Are there any of the written sequels that will change that? I don't think so either. Sweden's research policy is something we must continue to work on every day. Just because the Riksdag decides on the research bill is not the decisions we make today cut into stone. We'll keep working. I am proud of the research nation that Sweden is anyway. I am also proud that we have a study funding system and funding system that means that it is not the parents' wallet that determines who can become a researcher. Everyone has the financial opportunity, although we still have a lot to work with to ensure that we have equal and equal participation in higher education and research. The security of daring to invest in research has been put forward in our follow-up motion. The government has allocated the additional research resources decided in the budget proposal for this year. The Left Party fully supports the increased contributions that go to the higher education institutions as basic grants. We also support the focus on more searchable funds for free research, but I note that this part is not very large in the research bill and should be larger. It is a contribution to future budget proposals because I support the efforts made by the government on various programmes and, in some cases, even think they are at a disadvantage. This applies, among other things, to research on covid-19 and pandemics, where very much is to be contained within the 100 million in this report. The sum is the same as the investment in research on covid-19 last year alone. There is a reason to come back to that. I note that racism is not mentioned at all in the research bill, despite the fact that increasing racism and anti-Semitism are one of society's major challenges. The national research programme on racism, which was set up in connection with the previous research bill, does not have a large amount of money to allocate. There is also reason to consider extending the funding for research into racism and discrimination in the coming financial years. Another area where we believe the government is making too little effort is research on animal-free methods. Here the government writes very well, but you don't give any additions to the research. This is despite the fact that we know that animal-free methods are often cheaper, more efficient and safer for humans. Investing in research is therefore not a cost. It's an investment. The 15 million that are searchable is not very far enough for those researchers who want to develop alternative methods. In the Left Party Budget Movement, we doubled the searchable funds. There must be an end to animal suffering in the context of research, and then research methods must be developed. This is where the government gets back homework. Madam President, I would like We agree with the environmental orientation of the research bill. Sweden and the world are facing a climate crisis. Earth supplies us humans with the necessary natural resources, but they are not endless. The Left Party welcomes the strengthening of the national research programme on climate change and particularly welcomes the strengthening of the link between climate change and biodiversity. But even if we agree with the research proposal's focus on the environment, we believe that Sweden must highlight and invest in industrial research, something that is hardly mentioned in the research bill. We need to be better at targeting research funds to develop technologies that are crucial to climate change, not just targeting resources for research on climate change. If society is to be able to change, we also need to research techniques that facilitate this. In a number of areas, there are no strong proposals from the government. There is talk of the government's willingness to work further, that development is ongoing, that investigations are set up, and so on. I very much share the Government's ambitions in terms of creating better working conditions, making research more accessible and reproducible, getting an equal academy and much more. Nevertheless, I would like to highlight a number of proposals from the Left Party, which I believe should be supported by Parliament. One is an investigation of the teaching round to clarify the take-up of innovations so that responsibility for it does not lie solely with individual researchers. One is about the conditions for researchers with disabilities in universities and higher education institutions and measures to increase accessibility. One is about how support and conditions for postdocs can be strengthened and developed. One is about the universities' work to support doctoral students during the pandemic. One is an investigation into how to strengthen patent rights and reduce the imbalance between the parties in court proceedings. One is that the government's proposed research programme on crime should also focus on crime prevention. One is the proposal we have, together with the Christian Democrats, to set up a police research institute. Finally, we also have proposals for gender equality academy, which provides gender equality bonuses to universities – a maximum of 50 per cent of the research funding should go to men who do research – and for the bibliometrics to be investigated from a gender equality perspective. Of course, I support all of our motions, both those I have mentioned and those I have not mentioned, but I choose, in this debate, to only agree to reservation 38, which contains our equality proposals.

Today I would like to describe some of the most important proposals but also the risks that we see with some of the government's proposals. Madam President, I would like Solutions to societal problems and challenges have in the vast majority of cases emerged through creativity and knowledge generated by long-term investment in free research. As I mentioned earlier, the government provides significant resources to both universities and research councils. That's good. In total, the sector will receive between SEK 3.1 and 3.7 billion per year until 2024. But the government's detailed management of resources for state research funders is concerned. Most of the proposed resources are targeted at five cross-cutting areas: climate and environment, health and well-being, digitalisation, skills provision and working life, and democratically strengthened society. I believe, Madam President, that there are many of us who can agree with the government that these areas are urgent. These are societal challenges that we need to address. But the big problem is that the government is not content with identifying the framework. No, you go in with specific initiatives in each overall area. We Christian Democrats are in danger of this kind of detailed management leading to the risk that the search for answers to societal challenges might take place in the wrong place. In the long run, this can counteract Sweden's establishment as a leading knowledge nation. The Christian Democrats therefore believe that the vast majority of research funding should instead consist of support to safeguard free research. Madam President, I would like It is also essential that the research funding process is competitive and that the award is based on high scientific quality. This is guaranteed by the research funding system with the research councils and Vinnova. The Swedish Research Council is the largest of these authorities and has a well-developed and quality assured structure and process to ensure that the assessments of the applications are of high quality and carried out in an objective, impartial and transparent manner. However, there are also a number of authorities that allocate considerable sums of money to research and innovation, even though they do not have research funding as their main mission. These include MSB, the Swedish Environmental Protection Agency and the Swedish Transport Administration. The aim is for these authorities to finance research and innovation with a view to contributing to knowledge-based public activities. Madam President, I would like The aim is good, but the fact that the authorities do not have research funding as their main task puts state funds at risk of being allocated to research that is not of the highest quality. And it is clear that mediocre or poor research cannot help solve societal problems, no matter how good the purpose of research is. It is true that the Government announces in the bill that there are grounds for reviewing state research funding. It is welcome, but the government does not describe the principles on which the review should be based. From the Christian Democrats' side, we believe that it should be ensured that all state research funding is allocated through a quality process such as the Swedish Research Council. We also believe that the current research resources of these authorities without research funding should be released as the main task in favour of such a review process. Madam President, I would like The provision of secure forms of employment for young researchers is important to enable them to develop into prominent researchers. The government pays attention to the problem but unfortunately does not present any concrete measures to improve the situation. Government assignments in the form of surveys and follow-ups are not sufficiently capable of action, we believe. We also do not believe in the government's strategy of giving HEIs specific goals to increase the number of assistant lecturers. No, we believe that concrete measures are needed. While we welcome the fact that the government is raising the basic grant, we want part of that grant to be earmarked and used to ensure secure employment conditions for young researchers. It is also important to remove two concrete obstacles in the Higher Education Regulation in order for more people to be employed as assistant lecturers. I would therefore like to support the Christian Democrats' reservation number 31. We want the deadline for applying for a position as assistant lecturer to be extended from five to seven years after the public defence – this in order for more people to have time for merit and be able to be adequately evaluated. It is also important that the Higher Education Ordinance clearly states that a so-called "combined employment" is also available to persons who have a position as assistant lecturer. It is essential for new and better computer chips, cosmetics, textiles, food technology and pharmaceuticals. Research and development of this neutron science takes place, among other things, at the ESS facility in Lund. That being said, modern research is becoming increasingly dependent on advanced equipment. It is about large data capacity, cleanroom, advanced telescopes or accelerators. This is the infrastructure of research. During the alliance years, ESS just started in Lund. And we are pleased that within the framework of this proposal funds are being allocated both to research infrastructure and to long-term financing of precisely ESS. Finally, Madam President, it is wonderful that we have the opportunity to highlight both the need for education for a society and the importance of neutron science. The meanings are different, but a society needs both parts in order to develop. With this, I support the proposal in the report as a whole.
i-f2999c5907a82396-721	Madam President, I would like Sweden has a long tradition of being a prominent research nation. We are taking this tradition further today when deciding on the research and innovation policy bill presented by the MP-S government. It is a big and important day for Swedish research, for academia and higher education institutions, but also for the business community, civil society and all those affected when we take a comprehensive approach every four years to the focus of research, the conditions, funding and what innovation and collaboration should look like. I feel happy and genuinely proud of the government proposal Research, freedom, future – knowledge and innovation for Sweden. It is a product, a proposal, that takes responsibility and that meets the needs of the research and research community, but also from society, industry and industry, that meets the challenges we face and that helps to advance research in Sweden. Madam President, I would like The proposal takes several of the long-term initiatives initiated in the previous research bill further. This applies not least to the increased ambition for collaboration, but also to the long – ten-year – national research programmes. They have been successful and taken forward. Some of them will be strengthened, and some are added to this bill. They are based on the major societal challenges that we see. The threat to the climate, the environment and biodiversity is, of course, extremely important here. We have a great deal of knowledge in this area since before, but also very large and embarrassing knowledge gaps. The national research programme on climate has naturally grown to be the largest of these ten-year research programmes. We need to know more about how and when climate change affects us and our societies, how we can switch to some extent to counter climate change with technological solutions, changing lifestyles and reducing consumption. But there are also investments in what we call climate adaptation, i.e. research into what we need to do to meet the consequences of the changed climate. We know that climate change is here. We see effects in the form of drought, fires, floods, strong winds, disasters in the great but also deteriorating conditions for the individual in their agriculture and forestry. Already today we see that people globally need to move because the changed climate makes it impossible to provide or even live in certain places in the world. UNHCR and IOM expect 200 million people to have been forced to flee due to climate change up to the middle of the century – in 2050, in just three decades. This could almost be seen as the beginning of a new era of migration when so many people have to move. How do we face it while maintaining the idea of peace in the world? That's a big question. The threat to our livelihood, here in Sweden and globally, of food and drinking water are other major issues of destiny. A new ten-year programme on the sea and water is being set up to gain more knowledge about it. Biodiversity in our waters is under threat, and species are dying out. Large water reservoirs deep into the soil are emptied in anything but sustainable food production worldwide. Plastics and impurities of non-degradable substances are another threat to our watercourses. Madam President, I would like The health challenge is another part that is addressed in the bill. The pandemic has shown that we have shortcomings in health care, but it also reminds us that we need to live more healthy lives. A return to the way of life from which we come will not be able to cope, and neither will the health insurance system, for example, be able to do so. We need more research on, for example, public health. As far as health care is concerned, there is a great need for knowledge about antibiotic resistance, precision medicine and mental health, but also about specific diagnoses, rare diagnoses and diseases – as well as pandemics, of course. All these parts are included in the bill, with assigned funds. The same applies to primary care. We also need to know more about care injuries. It's something I've been running as a politician myself. It scares me terribly that 1 000 – 2 000 deaths every year are caused by mistakes in health care. A friend of my family, an elderly woman, passed away at Christmas after being hospitalized in a less serious condition. In the infirmary she was infected with covid-19, and she died shortly thereafter. I'm not gonna let that happen. The National Board of Health and Welfare has developed a national action plan on patient safety, which is largely about the need to expand our knowledge. There are funds allocated to the Forte Research Council for preventive measures and public health, which also mentions patient safety and should come in. Another area where the Green Party has been a driving force is alternative methods to animal testing. It has been highlighted earlier here in the debate, and a whole section of the bill has been devoted to it. Animal experiments are expensive, time-consuming and often imprecise, but today we need animal experiments to produce, for example, safe medicines and medicines. However, the same results can be obtained by alternative methods– in computer-generated simulations, through materials similar to human tissue or through organs on chips, for example. There is an EU directive that says we should phase out experimental animals. There is absolutely one ethical aspect in this area, but alternative methods can also reduce both costs and times for research. There is therefore a lot to be gained from this kind of initiative, and we already provide funding through the Swedish Research Council and 3R-center at the Swedish Board of Agriculture for this. In this context, I can also mention that the Swedish Board of Agriculture has now been given a mandate in the regulation letter to map measures to reduce the use of experimental animals. It is quite clear that we need an action plan, with deadlines. Madam President, I would like Are there any flaws in the bill? Yes, in some areas I think we might have come a little further. We are investing heavily in medical research and in innovation, technology and science, but we also need to continue investing in humanities. For example, we can see this in that the proportion of rejections in research applications in humanities and art is considerably higher than for other disciplines. Here I would have liked us to have come a little further in the bill, even if we make some investments in, for example, the education lift – which has meritoriously been lifted earlier in the debate – and research schools in the humanities. For the Green Party, the humanities are very important, and we are working on strengthening humanities science and its funding. We also need to do more to secure the employment of university teachers and researchers, as well as to create career paths and creative research environments. Today, we see that universities have an unreasonably large proportion of temporary employment, hourly employment, fixed-term employment and stacked employment. Here we need to make demands on the universities, and we do so in the proposal in the form of HEI-specific goals for career positions and for the recruitment of female professors. However, we have autonomous universities that themselves need to improve on these parts, and in the long run we probably also need to allocate more funds to this area. This is because we are also entering into academic freedom, which has, of course, been debated quite a lot recently – and also earlier here in the debate. If research is to be free, we need the opportunity to develop ideas freely. There is a need for environments with safe researchers and a close link between teaching and research; I think that is also important. In the bill, we raise the basic grants to the higher education institutions, and I hope to see that those funds are used for employment and creative research environments. Academic freedom is also strengthened by a new formulation in the Higher Education Act: It should be stated that acknowledgment of academic freedom should be promoted and protected. It's important and good – and called for, I guess I can also say. Threats and hatreds of scientists must really be curbed. It is a health and safety issue, but also a question of freedom of research and education. We are also seeing a decrease in the fundamental freedoms of countries around us. Democracy is in decline globally. Freedom of expression, including academic freedom, is part of what is restricted at an early stage in dedemocraticism. Here in Sweden, the threat comes with all the clarity from the Swedish Democrats. The government opens the bill for a possible future constitutional change. I would like to take the opportunity of the government to thank the Center Party and the Liberals for their very good cooperation. This proposal is important to Sweden as a leading knowledge nation. We increase resources for research and innovation by over SEK 3.7 billion per year from 2024 and over a four-year period by SEK 13.6 billion. This is a 10% increase in the level of government research funding to strengthen free research, to meet five broad societal challenges that are in line with the societal challenges identified by the OECD and the EU as well as to protect academic freedom. To meet societal challenges in terms of health and well-being, we make efforts in two crucial areas. On the one hand, we are establishing a new long-term national research programme on viruses and pandemics. We're investing in enhanced lab capacity, primary care and life science. On the one hand, we are making efforts to improve public health, including a new national research programme to combat mental illness. With regard to the societal challenge of climate and the environment, we are making a significant strengthening of the national research programme on climate change, which is now to become the largest national research programme. We are establishing a new long-term national research programme on sea and water. Sweden should take the lead in the field of the environment and climate. The work will be based on research and science, and this is a comprehensive strengthening of Swedish climate and environmental research. When it comes to skills provision and working life, which is the third societal challenge, we strengthen working life research. We propose a change of legislation that clarifies the responsibility of higher education institutions for lifelong learning, and we invest in research schools within health care and school and on an education lift. We advertise a series of measures to improve skills provision and secure conditions within universities and higher education institutions, which are the largest state employer. For the societal challenge of a democratic and stronger society, we are launching – again as with all national long-term research programmes – two new national research programmes that are long-term and ten-year-old in order to reduce crime and segregation and that involve a special effort on safe societies. Finally, for the last societal challenge, digitalisation, we are establishing a new national research programme on how digitalisation affects society. We are also investing in cybersecurity. This is particularly important now that the pandemic is accelerating digitalisation. We also invest in digital technology such as artificial intelligence. Of course, we will take advantage of the opportunities of digitalisation for better welfare, new jobs and enhanced competitiveness, but without people falling behind. In addition to these areas, a number of additional measures are being taken to strengthen research and innovation, including clarification of the collaboration task in the Higher Education Act and major investments in research infrastructure. We announce that Mälardalen University will become Mälardalen University. A new university in Mälardalen with significantly increased research resources is a strategic initiative to strengthen the region's development power and Sweden as the leading research nation. Mälardalen University has a prominent position among the country's higher education institutions. It provides a strong foundation to stand on. It is the university that has the highest number of doctoral students and the highest number of doctoral degrees. There is also a high proportion of external research funding. All these are quality indicators. Investments in research and education are obvious for Sweden's future. Knowledge builds Sweden stronger. The law on closed youth care as well as the law on care for young people means that the state is forced to override your freedom, either because you have committed a crime or because you are a danger to yourself. The question of how the state should respond, deal with and support minors who are criminals has been the subject of discussion for decades. In the 1970s and even before that there were, as we have heard described here, juvenile prisons where young people were placed after they committed crimes. They were placed indefinitely – to be released from juvenile prisons, they had to undergo a so-called written examination. But the passing of time led to the closure of juvenile prisons, and from 1979 the responsibility for young people convicted for crimes was transferred to the municipality. Some were placed in youth care schools, i.e. what is now Sis-hem. However, the relative transparency of Sis homes did not work for these young people with serious crime in their luggage. Some were still placed in prisons, in special youth departments, not least after criticism from the judiciary and the public that these young people did not work either in social services or in Sis homes. But even from time to time, in prison, the activity was not adapted to young people, and the criticism grew. Finally, in 1999, the Parliament decided on a special legislation for young criminals: LSU, the Act on closed youth care. The responsibility once again fell upon Sis. Thus, young people could take part in Sis' long tradition of care and treatment in a structured way. In fact, for many customers the electricity cost and taxes represent the majority of the amount of electricity bill. Madam President, I would like There is an urgent need to expand the capacity of the electricity grids, as the capacity shortage is a fact around the country and a growing problem. The Swedish Democrats agree with this. It is now so serious that it affects community development and planning and prevents, for example, housing projects, business start-ups and increasing electrification in order to achieve fossil freedom. In the long run, this leads to the loss of jobs as well as tax revenue for municipalities, regions and states, which goes against our well-being. The Swedish Democrats are fundamentally in favour of using unused deficits from previous revenue frameworks for investments that increase capacity in the electricity grid. For us, however, it is self-evident that there should be regulation that directs investment precisely there in order to create such specific drivers. We are not exactly alone in this view. Several consultation bodies agree with the criticism of lack of governance or want the proposal to be rejected completely. The energy authority is similarly positive but wants a number of conditions to be met. Consultation bodies The Swedish Energy Market Authority, the Swedish Competition Authority, the Confederation of Swedish Enterprise and the Swedish Municipalities and Regions fully reject the proposal in its current form. The Confederation of Swedish Enterprise believes that the proposal does not include a sufficiently clear customer perspective but rather leads to increased costs without adding more benefit to the user side. The Swedish Municipalities and Regions consider the proposal inappropriate and insufficient to remedy the local capacity shortage. The Swedish Democrats therefore believe that the Riksdag should reject the Government's proposal. Instead, the Government will come back with a proposal for a special investment space for electricity grid activities that provides increased incentives for electricity grid companies to invest in electricity grid capacity and where there are mechanisms to direct investments there. If the Riksdag nevertheless adopts the Government's proposal, which unfortunately appears to be the case, the Swedish Democrats believe that it is reasonable to evaluate after the first period of supervision how the Government's proposal has expired and whether the fees actually went to what was the purpose. The Government's proposal for a law states that such provisions may be made concerning the obligation for electricity grid companies to account in a special order for investments made in a accounting unit with special investment space. We do not think that is enough. It would have been more reasonable to have clearer guidelines on this and a letter from the Riksdag on such a follow-up to the proposal. There is a need for legislation that clearly requires what you want to achieve – instead of having to say years later that you have been naive and that this was not the case.
i-f2999c5907a82396-995	Madam President, I would like I request the approval of reservation 3. We also support the Swedish Democrats' reservation to reject the bill. What's this about? In short, the government wants to give electricity grid companies the opportunity to charge higher charges than they do today. The aim is to get more investment in the power grid, but few other than the government believe that it really is. Electricity customers will suffer from more expensive bills, but nothing suggests that the money will go to investments rather than even higher profits for the companies. The criticisms of the consultative bodies are stinging. Several consumer organisations, such as the Tenants' Association and Sweden's Public Benefits, but also the Swedish Competition Authority and the Government's own regulatory authority The Swedish Energy Market Inspectorate rejects the proposal. This is also the case for the Confederation of Swedish Enterprise and the Swedish Municipalities and Regions. The Energy Market Inspectorate, that is to say the government's own regulatory authority, states that electricity grid companies already receive cost recovery for their investments in the current regulation, which means that the proposal only leads to increased profits. The same conclusion is reached by the Tenant Association with several consultation bodies. The Competition Authority is as critical as several other consultation bodies. The Competition Authority considers that the proposal represents an unjustified increase in costs for electricity consumers as electricity grid operators are given the opportunity to increase charges without ensuring that the objective of capacity expansion is achieved. Electricity network companies have a monopoly on their markets. In other words, you cannot choose who supplies electricity to your property. And now for the strangest thing. When marketers constructed the system in 1996, it was promised that the network cost on the electricity bill would be lower. Instead, it was the opposite. The Swedish electricity market was liberalised, but only the electricity supply is competitive. Electricity distribution is a monopoly with 180 electricity grid companies spread throughout the country. The bill now under discussion is a revised version of the one presented by the government last autumn. At that time it was withdrawn because there was no support in Parliament. Now they have brought with them the Moderates, the Christian Democrats, the Center and the Liberals. Perhaps the bourgeois have a little remorse because their philosophy of life is that the market solves everything, and the liberalisation of the electricity market is something that can spell bourgeois politics. Friend of order naturally wonders if the bill is so much better now that you have received support for it in the Riksdag. I must admit that it has not become worse, but will it solve all the problems that have accumulated when electricity grid owners have neglected their commitments? The fact that investment has not been a priority, with all network charges being increased, is well known. When two thirds of the price increases made have been passed on to the parent company, robustness, capacity increases and so on have not been given priority. My answer will then be: No, it is not reliable enough. If we look at the ten largest network owners in 2015 we see that they made an operating profit of 8.7 billion SEK. In the period 2014 to 2019 electricity grid charges were increased by 27 per cent. There is nothing in society that has had such usury increases as network charges over a ten-year period. Then why is it like this? The electricity grid companies thus have a natural monopoly, and their way of charging customers is therefore regulated by the Energy Market Inspectorate, EI, which sets the framework for charging electricity grid companies can charge customers. When the Swedish Energy Market Authority has received new rules to calculate the so-called revenue framework for a period, known as the regulatory period, the majority of electricity grid companies have appealed against the revenue framework decision. In other words, electricity grid companies do not accept limited revenues. They refuse to comply with the Swedish Energy Market Inspectorate's decisions and try to secure future increases in electricity grid prices through litigation in court. It is these court proceedings that the government justifies its proposal, i.e. that since electricity grid companies still win in court, they need to be given the right to charge higher fees. I have to admit that the government has done the best it can to get a little more investment into the proposal that is on the table today, but the basic problem is that it is simply a system error. The closest I could compare this madness with is if we were to sell out all our state roads and then invent an authority to try to regulate road charges. The fact that a socially important function, as electricity networks are, has become a lucrative monopoly market which, moreover, does not work is, in any case, completely incomprehensible to me. Madam President, I would like Electricity distribution needs to be seen as part of society's infrastructure, which we jointly build on societal goals, such as other infrastructure, such as roads and railways. We cannot have a system where comparable home consumers can have SEK 7 000 in difference to the electricity bill on an annual basis, just because the monopoly market approves it. Electricity networks belong to basic infrastructure, and that is what society should take responsibility for.

Madam President, I would like Sweden has been a major mining nation for centuries because we have many mineral and metal bodies in our bedrock. Many of the mines are located in the northern part of the country and contribute significantly to the large increase in jobs in our northern part of the country. The mining industry is a very special industry, because its location is fixed and cannot be relocated. Examination of permits for mining operations is a process involving a large number of actors in several different stages. From an operator's intention to start searching for ore to starting a mine, several different permits are required. The review process differs from other environmentally hazardous activities in that, in addition to the Environmental Code, it also contains testing according to the Minerals Act. The environmental code test aims to promote sustainable development, while the mineral law test aims to enable society to supply essential metals and minerals. Licensing under the Minerals Act is designed to provide the conditions for applicants to reach mining by gradually adding increased financial input and knowledge to the project. From the time an operator wants to start looking for minerals to a possible start of mining operations, various trials are required. In addition to these trials, the operator may also need to apply for a permit for activities that may affect a Natura 2000 site. Other permits that may be required include exemptions from off-road driving bans, cultural memory protection, beach protection, species protection and biotop protection. If the operator plans a test break under a permit or processing concession, the environmental code requires a permit. Madam President, I would like What I'm trying to describe is a complex nutrition that takes time, is costly and complicated. Sweden has been at the top in mining industry for many years, but in recent years we have fallen on the ranking list because of all the regulatory problems and that it takes too long to get all the necessary permits. Sweden is dependent on a strong and driven mining industry, and there is a number of minerals in our bedrock that are not broken because the regulations are too complicated and it takes too long before you can get a profitable mining business. Our committee initiative, together with previous announcements, points out in many ways that we need to review all the regulations and make it much smoother and faster to seek permission without compromising either the environmental code or the mineral law. Growth analysis notes that national and European practices do not take into account the main challenge facing the mining industry, namely that unlike most other industrial establishments, operations are not delocalizable. Among other things, the Authority notes that the Swedish division of the authorisation process with concession testing and subsequent environmental permits does not meet the EU requirements for a comprehensive and complete environmental assessment required by the EU Directive for Natura 2000 sites. The Authority also notes that the EU practice developed in relation to Natura 2000, among other things, creates significant institutional and financial risks, which means that exploration and mining companies do not dare to invest in Sweden or the EU. Growth analysis already highlighted in 2018 that the state needs to clarify when the Natura 2000 test will take place. The Authority notes that the State should consider moving what is regulated in the Environmental Code regarding Natura 2000 trials to the subsequent environmental review. If an early Natura 2000 test is carried out, it cannot be complete, precise and final. A change in the mineral law should be made in line with EU law in order to make this possible. At the same time, the Authority points out that from an investment perspective it is important that as much as possible of the permit assessment, i.e. other land use testing, remains in place early in the permit process, i.e. in the concession trial. That is exactly what our committee initiative is about: making the necessary changes that take into account EU law but which take into account the risk for companies in general. Madam President, I would like There are ongoing investigations concerning the Minerals Act and the Environmental Code. None of these initiatives, however, specifically concern the issue of Natura 2000 permits. The committee therefore believes that ongoing work should be complemented by the issue of Natura 2000 permits. The Government should urgently return to the Riksdag with proposals that make it clear that in cases where a Natura 2000 permit is required, the examination of permits under the Environmental Code should be carried out. The committee therefore does not believe that a Natura 2000 permit should be a precondition for granting a processing concession. Therefore, the committee proposes that the Government should return with proposals as soon as possible, but no later than 30 June 2022, which coincides with the time when the Government will, according to the Riksdag's previous decision, return with proposals for simplified permit processes and shorter processing times. I therefore support the committee's proposal in the report.

This has happened, and today the Committee on Economic and Monetary Affairs is debating the Commission's communication on how they envisage a review of trade policy. The committee has now examined the communication. The Commission wants to reach a consensus on a new direction to address the challenges that exist globally with the experience gained during the Corona crisis. The Commission mentions that the challenges of the new era will respond to a trade policy for the 2030 world. To get there, you have identified five events that you want included. It is about globalisation and technological development, China's rapid rise, accelerating climate change, digitalisation and the economic outlook for the world as a whole. Three objectives have been added to this, which we want to see in the medium term. The objectives are: the EU's readiness to contribute to economic recovery through support for the green digital transition, a renewed focus on strengthened multilateralism and reformed global trade rules to ensure fairness and sustainability, a more determined approach to defending the EU's interests against the world, protecting the EU's economy against unhealthy trading practices and ensuring a level playing field for all. The Commission states that achieving the objectives requires a stronger commitment from the EU's global partners, and the USA is a key player. Madam President, I would like The Moderates broadly support what is stated in the European Commission's communication. Sweden should remain the main voice for free trade and openness within Europe and the world. We want to say this every time there is an opportunity. We therefore believe that the European Commission's ambition to open further negotiations on trade agreements is positive and important. Sweden is a small, trade-dependent country on the outskirts of northern Europe. We are a knowledge nation, and we have a lot to offer the global world in terms of business. We therefore share the Commission's view that it is good and important for the EU to conclude more free trade agreements. The EU needs to strengthen its strategic position so that trade policy contributes to both growth and sustainable development. The Commission's ambition to deepen trade relations with the neighbourhood and enlargement countries and increase synergies between the EU and Africa is welcome. In our neighbourhood there is Britain. We Moderates believe that safeguarding and maintaining trade between the EU and the UK should be a priority. The UK is an important trading partner for the EU but especially for Sweden. It is now important to ensure that the trade and cooperation agreement adopted last year works well for all parties involved. We welcome and believe it is urgent that the Commission's intention is to press ahead with ambitious negotiations on agreements and facilitate trade, market access and a level playing field. Protectionism in the world is on the increase. We know this with certainty, and it is a bad way for our world. The pandemic has clearly demonstrated what happens when value chains fail. That is why priority must be given to continuing and increasing free trade. The Commission's ambition to reform the WTO is therefore welcome. It is important that the EU stands up for open and rule-based trade. We therefore share the view that it is important for the EU to involve China in the reform of the WTO, although we believe that it is possible to conclude afterwards that it was a mistake to let them in. The regulatory international trade organisation is important to protect. We believe that the WTO is the right organisation to deal with global trade structures, but this is based on the fact that all Member States respect and comply with agreed guidelines and do not act in breach of the rules of the WTO. Total compliance is required of all. A WTO compliance code is therefore very important. Barriers and national special legislation that hinder the development of digital cross-border trade should be discouraged. The EU should therefore work towards reaching common rules at WTO level for e-commerce. Customs barriers in agricultural and industrial areas, as well as barriers to trade in services, must be combated. I would like to highlight the USA and say that we share the Commission's view that the new US Government is an opportunity to improve trade relations. It also provides an opportunity to work together to reform the WTO. We welcome the fact that the EU will give priority to ensuring its partnership with the US. I would also like to mention the trade agreement between the EU and the countries known as Mercosur: Argentina, Brazil, Paraguay, Uruguay and Venezuela. We believe that this trade agreement will benefit the economic development, growth and employment of both regions and that it is of strategic political importance. It ties countries closer together. We share the Commission's view that trade policy will play an important supporting role in the digital green transition. We also share the Commission's view that the EU's trade and investment agreements should contribute to the promotion of respect for human rights and other labour market rights. We also welcome the Commission's view on the importance of further increasing trade opportunities for developing countries in order to reduce poverty. Trade lifts countries. Madam President, I would like Last but not least, we would like to remind you that we do not believe that the European Pillar of Social Rights belongs in the review of trade policy. With reference to the above, I would like to support our reservation of reasons 1.
i-f2999c5907a82396-1257	Madam President, I would like World trade has been under pressure over the past year in the wake of the pandemic. We have seen how temporarily stopped production in mainly Asia had far-reaching consequences even here in Sweden when inputs to our domestic industry shone with their absence, so layoffs became a fact. We have seen how the urgent need for medical protection equipment across Europe led to protectionist features within the Union, in which states seized protective equipment belonging to someone else, and how export bans limited the trade patterns on which we depend to enable the distribution of important products. We have seen how Sweden's bad reputation regarding our handling of the pandemic has caused severe problems of border barriers between us and our Nordic neighbours, something that has not least been felt for the business community in connection with the Norwegian border and for all the Swedes who usually commute to Norway. We have seen how the EU's internal market has broken and how states outside the Union have both secured better access to vaccines and managed to vaccinate their own population more quickly. We have seen how the Swedish tourism and tourism industry has been on its knees as a consequence both of domestic recommendations and restrictions as well as of extremely limited tourism from abroad. The Commission communication on the review of trade policy should be seen in the light of the challenges and problems in this area that I have just outlined. When it comes to this communication, I can see that it consists largely of conventional snow mosh. The Commission is talking widely about an open, sustainable and determined trade policy. And as a consequence of the fluffy formulations, there is not too much to take on, either for criticism or praise. Nevertheless, I believe that the Swedish Democrats broadly support the spirit of the communication. Not least, it is positive that the Commission stresses the need for a reform of the WTO and a functioning dispute settlement mechanism. This is something that we have long been pointing out in our policy and lying on the government for it to work for. The communication's letters concerning the strengthening of the EU's focus on the implementation and enforcement of trade agreements are also something that we are very much in favour of. As you know, the Swedish democrats are generally in favour of free and open trade. Good trade relations are of great importance, and we therefore support the conclusion by the EU of more free trade agreements that promote growth both within and outside the Union. Our first reservation contains our position that we consider it important that Swedish interests are protected in free trade agreements and that the government actively takes a stand during negotiations for sectors where Sweden is leading. This is something that we believe can be done by, for example, trying to introduce things that strengthen Swedish comparative advantages in the agreements. Furthermore, we find it problematic that the Commission communication completely omits the EU's trade relations with the US. We believe that a good trade relationship with the US is of great importance, and we therefore welcome the USA's stated indications of transparency, participation and responsibility in future negotiations. The Commission should have included wording in the communication on the value of our trade relations with the United States and at least mentioned something about how we can deepen them in the future. One of the states mentioned in the communication, on the other hand, is China. And although the Commission has pointed to the challenges of the EU's trade and investment relations with China, it leaves out much more nuanced criticism of China and its actions. It is our view that China today represents the single largest foreign economic-political threat to Sweden and Europe. Clearer demands must therefore be placed on the Chinese regime and restrictions imposed on China's use of strategic tools, such as takeovers, government-backed investments, ownership of sensitive assets and financial control of Swedish companies. In order to maintain fair competition and protect non-Chinese operators, trade policy measures need to be taken to meet this distorted competition. Unfortunately, neither the EU nor the Swedish Government has acted sufficiently in this area. In our second reservation, we refer to further points that we believe the European Commission omitted in its communication. As far as trade and sustainability are concerned, we point out, among other things, that, when more free trade agreements are concluded, the EU should also be careful, as far as possible, to make demands on the countries' work on the environment, democracy and gender equality. In this way, the Union can strengthen both trade and democratisation while at the same time limiting aid efforts accordingly. We also believe that the EU should require states to receive their own citizens, where appropriate, by including readmission clauses in trade agreements. Non-economic interests should also be taken into account in trade policy. For example, it should be possible to impose the same requirements on animal products imported as on products produced domestically. In our second reservation, we also emphasise the importance of trade agreements covering areas such as corporate social responsibility and environmental aspects where the priority lies in reducing carbon leakage, without the EU implementing the social pillar and regulating detail either Swedish or foreign labour markets. Finally, we welcome the Commission's overall approach to giving priority to environmental and climate issues in the new trade policy. At the same time, we believe that the scope of the green man must be reviewed. The task of the green dealer is to promote long-term transition to sustainable and circular production. We stress in our reservation in this area that the EU's efforts to solve environmental and energy problems must contribute to better conditions for growth and increased prosperity. The implementation of the Green Paper must not create unnecessary administrative costs for businesses. The green transition must not be at the expense of jobs and must not hamper the competitiveness of businesses and thus reduce the opportunities for companies to be an important part of it. We also emphasise that we are opposed to the European Pillar of Social Rights and do not believe that it belongs to the review of trade policy. Madam President, I would like With reference to the foregoing, we recommend that the Parliament add the statement to the file in this section.

It is good that the EU is taking the first steps towards changing trade policy, but I am disappointed that the changes are not being made from scratch. On the contrary, in the ordinary EU spirit, a system is patched up, despite the fact that it clearly has not produced the global results that have been promised. We should not measure the success of trade policy solely in GDP figures and profits for companies based in the EU, but trade policy must help to promote societies globally. The indicators for this are provided by the global sustainability goals, which point to the positive development of countries in terms of gender equality, human rights, education, the abolition of child labour, democracy and biodiversity, for example. Success is achieved in cooperation with, not at the expense of, our global trading partners. Would it not be a modern trade policy – a policy aimed at complying with and achieving the international agreements to which we have committed ourselves, primarily within the environment, climate and human rights? That is why the Left Party wants a fair trade policy that puts people, social justice, animals, climate and the environment first and focuses on social benefits for all, not just for big business. As we rebuild our post-pandemic economy, we should use the opportunities for a consistent, environmental and social transformation of our economies, together with our partners in the world, to achieve the UN's global sustainability goals. It is about being able to mitigate climate change. Trade policy should become an instrument for this transition and not a tool for maintaining historical relations between Europe and the rest of the world. The Treaty is a way of holding nations accountable for what their companies do, even when they operate in other countries. This instrument is in a position to fill the legal gaps that exist, especially when transnational companies do not respect human rights. This has been a major problem when many of the transnational companies divide up activities and businesses in order to avoid taking responsibility on this issue.
