i-f2999c5907a82396-1441	Madam President, I would like We are here debating the management of spent nuclear fuel on the basis of the application to build a final repository submitted by Swedish Nuclear Fuel Management more than ten years ago. It is an issue that is now stuck with the government, despite a thorough process of preparation, and where it seems unclear when or how it will be able to move forward. The government is happy to talk about this as a matter that has recently been brought to its knees, but the issue is considerably older than that. Work on a final repository for spent nuclear fuel in Sweden has been ongoing since the 1970s, when the first geological studies were started to map suitable locations in Sweden. The localisation process led to the municipalities of Östhammar and Oskarshamn being suitable candidates to look at. Pre-studies and site surveys have been carried out from the mid-1990s. The respective municipalities have worked to build up expertise in the matter and have carried out ongoing reviews, which during the process have included civil society as well as politicians and expertise. It has also been possible for the public to visit the facilities where research for the final disposal method has been carried out. SKB has also submitted reports on research, development and demonstration of nuclear waste management and disposal methods every three years since 1986, i.e. for over 30 years. These reports are submitted to the Radiation Safety Authority, which examines them and gives its opinion to the Government. The government then decides whether the industry has implemented its F U D programme, which they are obliged to do. This programme is also submitted for consultation to some fifty organisations. The aim and purpose of this is to enable an application for disposal to meet the requirements against which it is examined. And, as I said, the application to build a repository was submitted back in 2011. In addition to the authorities' own experts, the evidence was examined by a large number of consultation bodies. The municipalities involved, county boards, universities, environmental organisations and others have all had to say what they think. The State also requested that an independent international review be carried out of the long-term safety after closure of the nuclear fuel repository. It was carried out by an expert group appointed by the OECD Nuclear Energy Agency, which delivered its final report in 2012 with a good rating. In January 2018, the Radiation Safety Authority and the Land and Environment Court handed over the application to the Government with their respective opinions, in which the Radiation Safety Authority endorsed the application for permission to build an encapsulation plant and a final repository for spent nuclear fuel. In June 2018, the Council of Oskarshamn decided to confirm that the Government grants admissibility to a spent nuclear fuel encapsulation plant in Oskarshamn. In the same month, the Ministry of the Environment also gave SKB the opportunity to supplement the application on the basis of the opinion of the Land and Environment Court, which was submitted on 4 April 2019. Last autumn, the council of Östhammar municipality decided to approve the application for final disposal of spent nuclear fuel in the municipality. In recent times, the Radiation Safety Authority has also presented a review report on the LOT trials that have been carried out. The overall assessment is that SKB has carried out the trials with high quality and that the results are reliable and in line with what can be expected based on established knowledge about corrosion processes for copper in the final user environment, which is also well in line with the trials that have been carried out in the past. Another good assessment of the work and research carried out in Sweden is that the Swedish model has also been approved by another government. In Finland, a final repository based on the Swedish KBS-3 method is to be built. The application to build the final repository in Finland was submitted in 2012, and in February 2015, Stuk, the equivalent of SSM in Sweden, submitted its opinion that they considered that the facility could be built safely. The government decision was taken since November 2015. Also to be mentioned is that the Ministry of the Environment has had a review team that has already worked on the matter for several years. In Finland, therefore, it took less than three years from the application to the decision of the government. In Sweden, the corresponding period is ten years. It took nine months for the Finnish Government to make decisions after receiving the evidence from its expert authority. In Sweden it has passed three times as much time. What remains is that the government must make decisions on permits under the Nuclear Technology Act and admissibility under the Environmental Code. But the government's response is missing, and you can't even answer if or when it will be given. That's beneath any criticism. It should reasonably be possible to present a timetable or which elements intend to look at – this in order to ensure that the process is as transparent as possible. Nor can we rule out the possibility of trying to divide the application into two parts, as this also involves the expansion of the intermediate spent fuel storage facility in Oskarshamn, which is part of the application. However, Oskarshamn municipality has opposed this. Worse still is the impact of the absence of a decision. This risks endangering the continued operation of Sweden's nuclear reactors when fuel changes are made impossible and the capacity of the intermediate warehouse hits the ceiling. The industry has made it clear that a decision is needed by this summer, but the government chooses to hold others hostage in this waiting period without providing any forecasts. Oskarshamn municipality has made it clear that it does not unconditionally support an increased capacity in Clab, but only stands up for it if the disposal and encapsulation facility receives an admissibility decision from the government. It is reasonable to consider that, for over 35 years, a major national responsibility has been taken for the temporary disposal of nuclear fuel and has repeatedly been mentioned during the process and in other application cases. Madam President, I would like The government often chooses to lean on the administrative authorities when it comes to different decisions and stresses the importance of this. In this case, they do not lean directly against their own expert authority, the Radiation Safety Authority, which has approved the application or the Land and Environment Court, which has said yes to the issues relating to the site at Forsmark, the mountain and the buffer, or the environmental impact assessment. Rather, they are undermined by saying that we are analysing whether something remains. What could these bodies have missed that cannot be told to the rest? It is important that such a process is conducted in an independent, transparent and transparent manner. I and the Swedish Democrats believe that this has been done in Sweden for a very long time and in a way that is quite unique and that impresses globally. My view is that there is sufficient evidence to enable us to decide on permits and admissibilitys. It is not the case that, once the decision has been taken, this will be released, as some in the debate are trying to assert. Conditions for the operation will be issued, and the activities of the final repository will be examined in stages, along with continued research. Madam President, I would like The important thing here and now is to move the process forward, and the government needs to make decisions on the issue of permits and admissibility in order for this to move forward. Our view is that at least a response to a timetable is deserved and which parts the government intends to look at. I support the committee's proposal.

Another aspect that is important is that the Center Party was very driven by Olof Johansson to push forward PPP, polluter pays principle, back in 1972. Then it fell heavily into the 1992 Rio conference. We are proud and pleased about it. Based on this, the F.D. process, research, development, demonstration, was formulated early on, which I think is very ingenious. This means that every three years SKB submits a report where everyone is involved – municipality, county board, radiation safety authority and environmental movement – and then the government says okay to this. It is a good and transparent process where everyone has been able to be on the train for a long time. Why do we have today's discussion? Because it is extremely important that we resolve the issue. Of course, I have great respect for the fact that you cannot rush a trial. I myself have worked in the Government Office and know that this is a difficult and extensive trial. At the same time, it has been there for three years, and it is always possible to prioritise the issue and move forward more quickly. Why is it so important? Well, it is so that if SKB is not told by summer, i.e. in a few months' time, you will not have time to build the repository. Then there will be a radical change in nuclear power production from 2024 onwards, because then there will be an end to intermediate storage capacity. The Center Party is no friend of nuclear power, and we are convinced that in 20 to 25 years' time we will be doing fantastically well without nuclear power. But if you are forced to fast stop in 2024, it is really bad for Swedish energy supply. We are also aware of this. That is why we are here today and call on the government to come to a decision. There's nothing else. Our instrument is today's debate and the committee report that we support. I very much hope that we will get a quick answer from, for example, Per Bolund next.

i-f2999c5907a82396-1513	Madam President, I would like Today we are debating an extremely important issue. This is an absolutely crucial matter. Sometimes I think that the heat of the battle may ignore the importance of the subject on which this debate is based. The question of how to handle Swedish nuclear waste is one of the biggest environmental issues that our generation has to deal with. It is a decision concerning one of the most absolutely dangerous substances known to man at all, i.e. highly radioactive nuclear waste. It is also a decision spanning a hundred thousand years. So long, the waste must be kept separate from humans and from our environment. This is simply not just a matter, nor should it be treated like any other. That is why I have to say that it is worrying about the speculation we hear from here – the rather wild guesses as to why the government is dealing with this matter in a serious way. For example, there have been a lot of misunderstandings about the history of this matter. Some pretend that the government would have made a decision on the matter in 1973 before any proposal was made. Others have pretended that the government would have been able to take decisions in 2011, when even the courts or our authorities had not taken a position on the application at all. The truth, Madam President, is that the government received this matter on 23 January 2018. This was when the Land and Environment Court and the Radiation Safety Authority, with their respective opinions, handed over the Swedish Nuclear Fuel Management AB's application for permission to construct, hold and operate installations in a system for the final disposal of spent nuclear fuel and waste. Then the matter came to the government, and this means that the government has had the application on its table for a little over three years – no longer than that. So let us not pretend to be anything else! It'll just be a game for the galleries. This application is handled with the same seriality as the government handles other administrative matters, and we do so in accordance with the principles of the Administrative Act. It includes that the government does not have a timetable for the examination in advance. There is no review by the government of all the hundreds of cases that land on the government table. To claim that there would be something unique in this case is also a game for the galleries. Something that makes this application very different, however, is that it is one of the biggest environmental issues in Sweden's history, and it is also a major infrastructure project. It includes shelves of material that has now been given to the government to enable us to make a well-prepared decision. What distinguishes this case in terms of action is that the case was not ready when the environmental court handed it over to the government. It is important to point out, because it was not the case that the court agreed to the application. When the Court of Justice submits a case concerning the authorisation of activities under the Environmental Code, the case is normally ready for decision. This was not the case this time. It does not help no matter how much the opposition pretends; it is nevertheless a fact that it was so. In its opinion to the Government, the Court stated that there were insufficient grounds for a decision. There were issues that had to be addressed in order for the operation to be allowed, and that is what the government has been dealing with while we have had the matter with us. It would not have been serious to – and we could not – take decisions because the Court said that there were issues that needed to be clarified. There were no answers. It is of great importance that the handling of matters should take the time needed for us to be sure that it is the right decision, and that is also why the legislator – the Riksdag – has put on the government table to make this kind of decision. The opposition is now pretending that the government is not going to make those decisions, but that it is enough for the authority to have said its thing, but it is not going to go the way of criticising the government because we are dealing with an administrative matter. If you are not satisfied with how the law is designed, you actually have to change the law. This is a decision that applies to a period of time that is almost staggering, and if we do not make the right decisions, the activity can have huge negative repercussions for an almost intransparable number of generations to come. We have a great responsibility to ensure that there are no such negative effects. The Land and Environment Court stated in its opinion, Madam President, that it believes that the government cannot allow the operation unless a number of outstanding questions are answered. Among other issues, SKB must be able to report that the final disposal facility meets the requirements of the environmental code in the long term, despite remaining uncertainties about the protection of the capsule and how it is affected by corrosion. It is also clear from the Court's position that it must be made clear who is responsible under the environmental code for the disposal facility in the long term, after closure. In view of the fact that the Court of Justice issued this opinion, the government has started a great deal of work, and it has been going on all the time since the case landed on the government's table. The Government requested that the application be supplemented by SKB, and SKB also submitted the requested supplement in April 2019. The completion involves, among other things, the ability to protect the capsule and aims to provide greater clarity about the uncertainties about corrosion on the copper capsule and how it affects the protective capacity of the repository. This complement was referred by the government in 2019, and then announced – according to the regulations we have to work on – the complement. It was in April, May and June 2020. So we are talking about last year, so it is not decades ago, as was claimed earlier in the debate. However, the Court not only left the matter of the corrosion impact on the copper capsule to the government to investigate further, but also looked at the question who is responsible for the storage in the long term after closure – when you are finished filling the repository. Therefore, the government launched a major effort to develop new legislation that was not in place, and the bill "A clarified state responsibility for certain nuclear activities" now clarifies the responsibility for the detention after closure. The Government then proposed a number of legislative amendments that entered into force on 1 November 2020. So we are talking about a few months ago. As decisions have now been taken, it has been made clear that the responsibility for the final disposal facility under the Environmental Code lies with the State in the long term. So that clarification did not exist until then. The task of the government in the examination is to examine the application in its entirety. SKB's application to build, possess and operate a system for the disposal of spent nuclear fuel and waste is a major and complex issue, as the representative of the Center Party admitted here in the speaker's chair. There we should be able to agree that it is a large and complex matter. A question that has also arisen with the Government in the examination of the application is whether the complement that SKB submitted to the Government in response to the Court's opinion regarding, among other things, the protective capacity of the copper capsule requires an additional consultation with other countries, a so-called Espoo consultation, to which we are bound by the conventions that Sweden, including by decision of the Riksdag, has entered. The Environmental Protection Agency, which is the authority responsible for conducting consultations with other countries in accordance with the Environmental Code and the Espoo Convention, was therefore asked to inform the Government Office of whether or not the complement would lead to a consultation. The Environmental Protection Agency replied that the Authority intends to hold such a consultation on the matter, and this is done with Poland and Germany, which has also expressed a clear desire to take note of developments in the case. This consultation is currently under way. Making decisions before the consultation is concluded would violate one of the conventions that Sweden has signed. Then the question must go back to the Members of Parliament. When, with pretended or genuine indignation, one now thinks that the government is dragging its feet, does the majority of the parliament mean that this decision should be made before the consultation is completed? We would then be in breach of the Espoo Convention. Or does Parliament mean that we should leave the Espoo Convention and that Sweden should not be in it anymore? It is quite difficult to understand what the Riksdag really means. Another issue that has arisen during the time that the government's review has been going on has to do with the Radiation Safety Authority publishing its analysis of SKB's report on the research known as Long Term Test of Buffer Material, the so-called LOT trials. The Government is now going through the Radiation Safety Authority's review, which we are also expected to do according to the legislation decided by the Riksdag. Madam President, I would like I would like to say a few words about the role of the government in the review in relation to the Land and Environment Court and the Radiation Safety Authority, because that issue has also been raised. The government review includes a licence review under the Nuclear Activities Act, the Nuclear Technology Act, and refers to the conditions for the planned installations to meet the radiation safety requirements necessary to protect human health and the environment against the harmful effects of ionising radiation. The Government is examining site and method choices with regard to long-term safety and also safety in construction, operation and closure. The government review also includes an admissibility test under the Environmental Code, which Members seem to forget. It means that the government should decide whether the operation can be carried out at all. The activities eligible for governmental review are those of such a significant scale or intervention that they typically affect important social interests. It is therefore the Riksdag that has decided that it is the government that will make this test. Nuclear installations tested by the Government under the Act on Nuclear Activities are subject to so-called mandatory admissibility testing. That is to say, the government must always decide whether this type of activity can be allowed under the environmental code. If the Government permits activities, the Land and the Environment Court shall then grant permission for the activities and lay down the detailed conditions applicable to the activities. It is therefore not that it is a court or authority that makes that decision, but that the parliament has decided that the government will do the examination. The Government has undertaken to follow the principles expressed by the Administrative Act in such trials in the handling of administrative matters. If Members had read that law, they would have been able to see that this would mean that the cases would be dealt with as simply, quickly and efficiently as possible without the rule of law being respected. The committee initiative is therefore exactly what the Administrative Act already says. It must be said, however, that the door is completely wide open. The question is also why the Riksdag first decides that the Government will examine a matter and then, with real or recorded indignation, is upset that the Government is doing exactly what the Riksdag has decided through the legislation that we should do, i.e. examining a matter. To conclude, Madam President, I would like to emphasise once again that the government is, of course, working on this issue as a matter of priority and as a matter of urgency, preparing a decision. However, as I said, and as in all administrative matters, as Minister, I cannot anticipate the decision or exclude or exclude certain procedural measures in advance. It is precisely in this way that the government handles all the trials that come in. Therefore, I do not think that there is any reason for the majority's announcement either, but I am happy to hear how the Members of Parliament justify that announcement.

i-f2999c5907a82396-1646	Madam President, I would like I grew up living in Oskarshamn. I feel lucky and love this district. I am also a member of Oskarshamn City Council, as I am a member of the Swedish Parliament. As an oskarshamner, I have followed and been involved in the nuclear waste issue since my teens. There are many of us who have spent a lot of time on this through schools, workplaces, living environments, associations and other forums. We have had extensive municipal work on nuclear waste through our local, independent competence building project. For decades we have been there for Sweden. With us is the entire country's highly active spent nuclear fuel, which has provided electricity and prosperity to everyone throughout the country. Madam President, I would like In 2011, Swedish Nuclear Fuel Management, SKB, applied to the Swedish Radiation Safety Authority, SSM, and the Land and Environment Court to build a disposal facility for spent nuclear fuel in Östhammar Municipality, an encapsulation facility for spent nuclear fuel in Oskarshamn Municipality, and an expanded storage of nuclear fuel in the Central Reservoir for spent fuel, Clab, in Oskarshamn Municipality. It is now ten years ago. The government has had the question on its table since the last parliamentary term, when the SSM and the Land and Environment Court issued their opinions. After decades of work in and by the municipalities, the examination of the facilities has come so far that both municipalities, Östhammar and Oskarshamn, have left their strength to build the facilities to the government. That is where the issue is now settled pending a decision by the government. Madam President, I would like Oskarshamn municipality has followed the work through our local review team. The nuclear waste issue has taken a lot of time from the municipal board and the municipal council, not to mention our municipal administration. Of course, this has had displacement effects. But we have been honoured to take this issue very seriously since the 1970s. We have invested a lot of time and money in taking our share of responsibility. It is this long-term, serious and deep work that has led to our strength of application. It is based to a very large extent on the fact that the Radiation Safety Authority, SSM, has approved SKB's methods and assessed that a final disposal facility and an encapsulation facility can be built in a long-term safe manner. SSM will continue to gradually test SKB's research and construction of the facilities before SKB will start operating them in the 2030s. We are safe with this order. We are also confident that the application will provide a system for managing the entire nuclear waste cycle. The system is based on the fuel being encapsulated in connection with Clab and from there transported to the final repository in Östhammar when that facility is completed. But in the application there is also the question of expanding the storage in Clab as it is needed to store more fuel. By 2023, Clab will be full if nothing is done. Since Clab is not a final repository, Oskarshamn does not unconditionally increase its capacity in Clab alone. We have made that clear. Since the start of the intermediate warehouse in 1985, we have said that this is not a long-term sustainable solution for Sweden's nuclear waste. Oskarshamn municipality was also then social democraticly governed, and our position is well known, both in Sweden and in our party. We have been there for Sweden and for the government on this issue, regardless of the party colour, and we have taken a major national responsibility. We have always been actively involved in the process of making a repository a reality. We have always stressed that Clab is an intermediate warehouse and must not be transformed into a final repository in practice. Madam President, I would like Let us go back to the last millennium, when the government decided in 1998 on increased storage capacity in Clab. The Environment Minister was then named Anna Lindh. The government decision clearly shows that the government took note of our position at the time. I quote from the government decision of 20 August 1998: Oskarshamn municipality has stated that it wants to see a safe final disposal developed and will not accept an extended storage in Clab beyond the intended period of time. This is just one example of many where we know that our message to the government has been made. It couldn't have gone by. We have repeated it over time, and I can assure you, Madam President, that we have repeated it intensively over the past year. We do not accept that Clab should be expanded to de facto become a repository. The extension must go hand in hand with final disposal decisions. We put security first. We are confident that our party colleagues in both the Defence Committee and the Government Office are aware of this position and share our view that the issue must be dealt with urgently and collectively. Madam President, I would like Let me repeat these key words: quickly and together. The matter should be dealt with urgently because the current application has been reviewed by Swedish and international experts for ten years and the government has had it on its table since the last term of office, because Oskarshamn municipality's position has been known for decades and that if the government does not act, great values are at stake for our country. It should be handled together because Clab is an intermediate layer, because it cannot be expanded without seeing a final repository in sight and because a broken, shared process at this stage is not a serious handling of the issue after decades of work. If the government intends to run over Oskarshamn's municipality in this matter, break up the process and thereby make the intermediate warehouse in Oskarshamn municipality a long-term detention, in practice a kind of disposal, it is the first time in Swedish history that the state and government are driving over a local community in a nuclear installation. Historically, the government has listened to local communities on issues of nuclear installations. If consensus has not been reached, the government has backed, listened and tried to find other ways. The keywords have been a lot of together and a lot of respect for proximity. This has been the case until recently, when Oskarshamn Municipality receives a reply from the Government Office and the Department responsible. It has gone so far as to require a formal exchange of letters for the municipality to receive answers, and even then not all answers are given. On the contrary, it is claimed that there are new questions, but no one tells what questions require answers. It is a break in how respectful communication has previously worked between the state and the municipality. And that, Madam President, is something I, as a member of the Constitutional Committee, note with concern. Madam President, I would like I must also say that it would be remarkable if it were a government with some who call themselves an environmental party, which would thus be the first to run over a local community in this way. I would like to remind the President and everyone else in this House that territorial responsibility is a regulated municipal task. We stand up for our community and our citizens. One of MP's representatives in this debate said last week at a meeting where all parties were invited that it would be Oskarshamn municipality's fault if nuclear power shuts down. Madam President, I would like As the President understands, I would strongly reject such a statement of reality. It is not serious of the MP to treat us in the local community in this way. We have been clear since the start. Clab's not a disposal facility. It's an intermediate storage facility. We have reiterated that the government must act in good time before Clab becomes full in order to start building a repository. We will continue with this. We hope that we as a local community will be heard by the state of Sweden and that the central government, in this case MP, will show us respect. In this context, I would like to say that our municipality has close and very good cooperation with the municipality of Östhammar. We appreciate their open support for our stance. Madam President, I would like We will not accept the government turning Clab into a long-term repository. It is not intended for this, and it is therefore not a safe approach. We stand up for an increased storage if the disposal facility and the encapsulation facility receives an admissibility decision from the government. Sweden has a long and good administrative tradition of the government and parliament relying on their independent expert authorities for decision-making. Now, two municipalities, an authority and a court have agreed that the disposal and encapsulation facility can be built. Remains only make the government's decision so that the work can continue. Madam President, I would like To conclude, I would like to take this opportunity to clarify a few things that sometimes occur in the debate; it becomes a mixed compote. Firstly, the question of disposal is not about for or against nuclear power. After several decades of nuclear power, there is now a waste that needs to be handled. Now the question is at the government's table. Secondly, in response to the signals from the nuclear industry that they may have to shut down production if the government does not take a decision on the question of final storage, the debate sometimes reappears that it is up to the producers themselves to ensure that they have the necessary permits. This is a sad circular argument, because it is precisely because companies want to ensure that they have the necessary permits that they point to the responsibility of the government. Thankfully, we are living in a political democracy in which companies cannot decide for themselves what to do with environmentally hazardous waste, but it requires permission – in this case now from the government after two municipalities, one authority and one court approved the proposal, as I said. Thirdly, something that recurs is that there is no harm to more research and to investigate the issue more and that this would be an argument for waiting for decisions. We agree that research is necessary. That is why, since the 1970s, we have been pressing for this and have been most involved. But the fact is that since Clab is an intermediate layer, not a repository, a non-decision actually means that it can lead to harm. More research is welcome, but this does not prevent the government's decision. Even after a decision, the step-by-step test continues. Should something arise that means that the plan must be revised, there is time to do so. The first capsule will not enter the ground until about a decade, at the earliest. Last of all, Madam President, we Members of Parliament are running on our party lists. We represent the whole of Sweden and everyone who lives here. But we are also elected in our constituencies. It is not for nothing that we in this House are divided constituency-wise, not party-wise. It's a fine order of thought. I feel very confident when, on the basis of both my membership as well as my Social Democratic party membership and my geographical residence in Oskarshamn and Kalmar County, I say that it is high time to make a decision.
